On the 26th of January 1857, the Master of the Rolls 
submitted to the Treasury a proposai for the publieation 
of znaterials for the History of this Country from the 
Invasion of the Eomans to the reign of Henry Vili. 

The Master of the RoUs suggested that these material» 
should be selected for publication under competent 
editors without reference to periodical or chronological 
arrangement, without mutilation or abridgment, prefer- 
ence being given, in the first instance, to such materials 
as were most scarce and valuable. 

He proposed that each chronicle or historical docu- 
ment to be edited should be treated in the same way as 
if the editor were engaged on an Editio Princeps ; and 
for this purpose the most correct text should be formed 
from an accurate coUation of the best MSS. 

To render the work more generally useful, the Master 
of the Eolls suggested that the editor should give an 
account of the MSS. employed by him, of theìr age and 
their peculiarities ; that he should add to the work a 
brief accotmt of the life and tìmes of the author, and 
any remarks necessary to explain the chronology ; but 
no other note or comment was to be allowed, except 
what might be necessary to establish thecorractness of 
the text. 



é 

Tàe Works to be published in octavo, separately, as 
they were finished ;^ the whole responsibility of the task 
resting upon the editom, who weré to be chosen by the 
Master of the EoUs with the sanction of the Treasury. 

The Lords of Her Majesty's Treasury, after a careful 
consideration of the subject, expressed their opinion in a 
Treasury Minute, dated February 9, 1857, that the pian 
recommended by the Master of the Bolls "was well 
calculated for the accomplishment of this important 
national object, in an effectual and satisfactory manner, 
within a reasonable time, and providedproper attention be 
paid to economy, ia making the detaUed arrangements, 
without unnecessary expense." 

They expressed their approbation of the proposai tha t 
each Ohronicle and historical document should be edited 
in such a manner as to represent with ali possible correct- 
ness the text of each writer, derived from a collation of 
the best MSS., and that no notes should be added, except 
such as were illustrative of the various readings. They 
suggested, however, that the preface to each work should 
contain, in addition to the particulars proposed by the 
Master of the E/olls, a biographical account of the author, 
so far as authentic materials existed for that purpose, 
and an estimate of his historical credibility and vaine* 

jRolls House^ 

Decemher 1857.

Anno gratiae MCCVii. rex Johannes celebravit natale 
Domìni apud Wintoniam, prsesentibns magnatibus 
regni. Deinde in purificatione beatse Mariae cepit per 
totam Angliam tertiam decimam parfcem ex omnibus 
mobilibus et rebus aliis, tam de laicis quam viris 
eeclesiasticis et praelatis, cunctis murmurantibus sed

episcopus Eboracensis, non consentiens, sed piane con- 
tradieens, elanculo recessit ab Anglia, et in reeessu 
suo omnes speeialiter, qui in archiepiscopatu Ebora- 
censi hanc rapinam facerent, et in genere universos 
ecclesia3 sive rerum ecclesiasticarum invasores, anathe- 
matis sententia innodavit. 

Eadém tempestate circa noctem mediam ventus 
vehemens et repentinus superveniens asdificia diruit, 
arbores prostravit, oves et amienta exaggeratis nivibus 
sexto kalendas Februarii interire coegit.

Ut rex Johannes exercitum suum in Britanniam 
promoverit 

Sub diebus istis Johannes, rex Anglorum, promovit 
exercitum suum a Pietavia in minorem Britanniam, 
continuis tribus diebus cum totidem noctibus. Veniens 
tandem prope civitatem, qua? Nantes ab incolis appel- 
latur, eam debellare disposuit; sed cives et milites, 
quos in ejus prsesidio rex Francorum posuerat, cum 
regis [Anglorum] adventum cognovissent, exierunt 
obviam ei, et in introitu cujusdam pontis, qui non 
longe ab urbe distabat, cum exercitu regis Anglorum 
congressi sunt; sed rex Johannes, Marte sibi propitio, 
victoriam tandem adeptus viginti milites ^ in ilio con- 
flictu cepit, et Inter cseteros filium et hseredem comitis 
Roberti de Druis et avunculi Francorum regis vinculis 
constrictum secum rediens abduxit. Deinde idem rex 
ad Rupem-monachi cum exercitu suo se conferens 
castrum obsidione vallavit; quod audiens Lodowicus, 
regis Francorum filius, ad hoc a patre missus, ut regis 
[Johannis^] reiraenaret incursus, cum exercitu non 
modico illuc properavit, ut subveniret obsessis. "Cum- 
que adventum ipsius rex Anglorum cognovisset, misit

De electione magistri Simonis de Langetuna ad 
ecclesiam Eboracensem.

As far back as the yeàr 1800, a Oommittee of the House of Commons recom- 
mended that Indexes and Calendars should be made to the Public Becords, and 
tbirty-six years afterwards another Oommittee of the House of Oommons rei te- 
rat ed that recommendation in more forcible words ; but it was not unill the 
incorporation of the State Paper Office with the Public Record Office that the 
Master of the Rolla found himself iti a position to take the necessary steps foi* 
carrying out the wishes of the House of Commous. 

On 7 December 1855, he stated to t)ie Lords of the Treasury that although 
*' the Records, State Papers, and Documents in his charge constitnte the most 
** complete and perfect series of their kind in the civilized world," and although 
** they are of the greatest vaine in a historical and constitutional point of view, 
** yet they are comparatively useless to the public, from the want of proper 
•* Oalendars and Indexes.** Acting upon the recommendations of the Oom- 
niittees of the House of Oommons above referred to, he suggested to the Lords 
of the Treasury that to effeot the object he had in view it would be necessary for 
him to employ a few Persons fnlly qualified to perform the work which he con-

THE CHRONICLES AND MBMORIALS OF GREAT BRITATK 
AND IRELAND DFRING THE MIDDLE A6ES. 



[ROTAL 8vo» half-bound. Priee IO5. each Volume or Part.] 



On 25 July 1822, the House of Gommons presented an address to the Crown, 
stating that the edifeions of the works of our ancìent historians were inconvenient 
and defectire ; that many of their writings stili remained in mannscript, and, in 
some cases, in a single copy only. They added, **thatan uniform and con- 
** venient edition of the whole, published under His Majesty^s royal sanction, 
** would be an underfcaking honourable to His Majesty*s reign, and condticive to 
** the advancement of historical and constitutional knowledge ; that the House 
** therefore humbly besonght His Majesty, that He would be graciously pleased 
*' to give such directions as His Majesty, in His wisdotti, might think fit, for 
*' the publication of a complete edition of the ancient historians of this realm, 
** and assured His Majesty that whatever expense might be necessary for this 
** pnrpose would be made good." 

The Master of the EoUs, being very desirous that effect should be given to the 
resolution of the House of Commons, submitted to Her Majesty 's Treasury in 
1867 a pian for the publication of the ancient ohronicles and memorials of the 
United Kingdom, and it was adopted accordingly. In selecting these Works, it 
was considered right, in the first instance, to give preference to those of which 
the manuscripts were unique, or the materials of which would help to fili up 
blanks in English history for which no satisfactory and authentic information 
hitherto existed in any access ible form. One~great object the Master of the Bolls 
had in view was to form a corpus Mstoricwm within reasonable limits, and which 
shonld be as complete as possible. In a subject of so vast a range, it was im* 
portant that the historical student should be able to select such rolumes as 
conformed with his own peculiar tastes and studies, and not be put to the expense 
of purchasing the whole collection 5 an inconvenience inseparable from any other 
pian than that which has been in this instance adopted. 

Of the Ohronicles and Memorials, the following volume s bave been published. 
They embrace the period from the earlìest time of British history down to the 
end of the reign of Henry YIL

The first volume contains originai matemls for the history of the setfclement of the order of

5. Fasciculi ZizANioKUM Magistbi Johannis WycIiIF cum Tritico. Ascribed to

This work derives its prìncipal value from being the only contempoi*aneous account of the rìse 
of the LoUards. When wrìtten, the dispute» of the schoolmen had been extended to the field of 
theology, and they appear both in the writìngs of "Wycliff and in those of his adversaries. Wycliff *s 
little bundles of tare» are not less metapbysical than theological, and the conflict between Nomina- 
lists and Realìsts rages side by side with the conflict between the different interpreters of Scripture. 
The work gives a good idea of the controversies at the end of the léth and the beginning of the 15th 
centuries.

, century. The narrative begins with the earliest legends and end» with the death of James I. of 
Scotland, and the "evil endins: of the traitors that slew him.** Stricfc accuraoy of statement is not 
to be looked for ; but the stories of the colonization of Spain, Ireland, and Scotland are interesdng 
If not true ; and tho chronicle reflects the manners, sentiments, and character of the a^e in which 
it was composed. The peculìarities of the Scottish dialect are well illustrated in this version» 
and the studeut of language will flnd ampie materials for comparison with the English dialects of 
the same period, and with modem lowland Scotch.

This work is dedicated to Henry VI. of England, who appears to have been, in the author's

name of Henry in various parts of the world. Canove was born in 1393, in the reign of Richard 
II., and lived during the Wars of the Boses, for which period his work is of some value.

19. The Repeessob. of over huch Blaming op the Olergit. By Eeginald I

The'*Bepressor*' m&y be considered the earliest piece of good theological disquisition of 
which our English prose literature can boast. The author was bom about the end of the four- 
teenth century, consecrated Bishop of 8t. Asaph in the year 1444, and translated to the see of 
Chiehester in 1450. While Bishop of St. Asaph, he zealously defended bis brother prelates froia 
the attacks of those who censured the bii^hops for their neglect of duty. He maintained that ifc 
was no part of a bishop's functions to appear in the pnlpjt, and that his timo might be more profi- 
tably spenti and his dignity better maintainfed, in the performance of works of a nigher character. 
AmoiJg those who thought differently were the LoUards, and agaiiist their general doctrines the 
** Repressor *' is directed, Pecock took np a position midway between that of the Roman Church 
and that of the modem Angìican Ohurch ; but his work is interesting chiefly because it gives a 
fvill account of the views of the Lollards and of the arguments by which they were supported, and 
because it assista us to ascertain the state of feeling which ultimately led to the Beformation. 
Apart f rom religious matters, the light thrpwn upon contemporaneous history is very small, but 
the ** Repressor " has great vaine for the philologist, as it tells us what were the oharàcteristics of 
the longuage in use among the cultivated Bnglishiaen of the fifteenth ©entury. Pecock, though an 
opponent of the Lollards, showed a certain spirib of toleration, for which he reoeived, towards the 
end of his life, the usuai medigeval reward— pèrsecutjon.

Neckam was a man who devoted himself to scìence, such as it was in the twelfth century. 
In the ** De Naturis Rerum " are to befomid what may he called the rudiments of many Sciences 
mixed up with much error and ignorance. Neckam was net thought infallible, even hy his 
contemporarles, for Roger Bacon remarks of him, " This Alexander in many things wrote what was 



** true and u&ef ul ; but he ueither can nor ought by just title to be reckoned among authorities.' 
Neckam, however, had sufflcient independence of thought to differ trom some of the schoolmen 
who in hìs time consìdered themselves the only judges of literature. He had his own views in 
morals, and in giving us a glimpse of them, as well as of his other opinions, he throws much 
light upen the manners, customs, and general tono of thought prevalent in the twelfth century. 
The poem entitled " De Laudibus Bivinse Sapientise '* appears to be a metrica! paraphrase or 
abridgment of the " De Naturis Rerum." It is written in the eiegiac metre, and though there are 
many lines which violate classical rules, it is, as a whole, above the ordinary standard of mediseval 
Latin.

DOMBSDAY BOOK, Or the G-HBAT SuRVEt OF EnGLAND OF WiLMAM the GONQTrEBOK, 

1086 ; fao-simile of the Part relating to each county, separately (with a few 
exceptions of doublé counties). Photozincographed, by Her Maje'sty's Oom- 
mand, àt the Ordnance Survey Office, Southampt-on, Golonel Sir Henby 
James, B.E., F.R.S», &c., Director- General of the Ordnance Survey, under 
the Superintendence of W. Basevi Sanders, Esq., Assistant Keeper of 
Her majesty*s Records. 35 Parts, imperiai quarto and demy quarto 
(1861-1863), boards. Price Ss, to 11. Zs. each Part, according to size ; or, 
bound in 2 Vols., 20Z. (The edUion in two volumes is out ofprint.) 

This important and onique survey of the greater portion of Englaiid* is the 
oldest and most valuahle record in the natìonal archìves. It was commenced 
about the year 1084 and finished in 1086, Its compilation was determined 
upon at Gloucester by William the Conqueror, in council, in orderthat hemight 
know what was due to him, in the way of tax, from his subjects, and that each 
at the same time might know what he had to pay. It was compiied as much 
for their protection as for the benefit of the sovereìgn. The nobility and people 
had been grievously dìstressed at the time by the kmg bringing over largenum- 
bers of Prench and Bretons, and quartering them on his subjects, **each 
*' according to the measure of his land," for the purpose of resisting the invasion of 
Chut, King of Denmark, which was apprehended. The Comm issioners appointed 
to roake the survey were to inquire the name of each place j who held it in the 
time of King Edward the Confessor ; the present possessor ; how many hides 
were in the manoi* ; how many pio ughs were in the demesne ; how many homagers j 
how many villeins ; how many cottars ; how many serving men ; how many free 
tenants ; how many tenants in soccage ; how much wood, meadow, and pasture ; 
the number of mills and fish ponds ; what had been added or taken away from 
the place ; what was the gross vaine in the time of Edward the Confessor ; the 
present vaine ; and how much each free man or soc-man had, and whether any 
advance could be made in the value. Thus could he ascìrtaiued who held the 
estate in the time of King Edward ; who then held it ; its value in the time of 
the late King ; and its value as it stood at the forraation of the survey. So 
minute was the survey, that the writer of the contemporary portion of the Saxon 
Ohronìcle records, with some asperity — ^** So very narrowly he caused it to be 
" traced out, that there was not a single bidè, nor one virgate of land, nor even, 
*< it is shame to teli, though it seemed to him no shame to do, an ox, nor a cow, 
** nor a swine was left, that was not set down." 

Domesday Survey is in two parts or volumes. The first, in folio, contains the 
counties of Bedford, Berks, Bucks, Cambridge, Chester, and Lancaster, Corn- 
wall, Derby, Devon, Dorset, Gloucester, Hants, Hereford, Herts, Huntingdon, 
Kent, Leicester and Butland, Lincoln, Middlesex^ Northampton, Nottingham, 
Oxford, Salop, Somerset, Stafford, Sùrrey, Sussex> Warwick, Wilts, Worcester, 
and York. The second volume, in quarto, contains the counties of Essex, 
Norfolk and Snffolk. 

Domesday Book was printed verbatim et literatim during the last ceutury, in 
consequence of an address of the House of Lords to King George III. in 1767. 
It was not, however, commenced until 1773, and was completed early in 1783. 
In 1860, Her Majesty's Govenmient, with the concurrence of the Master of the 
Bolla, determined to apply the art of photozincography to the production of a 
fac-simile of Domesday Book, under the superintendence of Colonel gir Henry 
James, R.E., Director-General of tìie Ordnance Survey, Southampton, The 
fac-simile was completed in 1863. .

land, \f estmorelaud, and Dui^am, are not described In the survey \ nor does Lancashirc appear under 
its propei^ nante ; but Funtess, and the northem part of Lanoashire, as well as the sonth of Westmore-

This work fonns a comprehensive Palseographic Series for Ireland. It 
f amishes characterìstic specimens of the documents whieh bave come down 
from each of the classes "which, in past ages, formed principal elements in the 
popnlation of Ireland, or exercised an influence in her afiairs. With these 
reproductìons are combìned fac-similes of vritings connected with emìnent 
personages or transactions of importance in the annals of the country to the 
early part of the eìghteenth century. 

The specimens bave been reproduced as nearly as possible in accordance with 
the originala, in dimcnsions, colouring, and general appearance, Characteristic 
examples of styles of writing and caligrapbic ornamentation are, so far as 
practicable, associated with subjects of bistoric and linguistic interest, 
toescriptions of the various mannscripts are given by the Editor in the Intro- 
duction. The contenta of the specimens are fully elucidated and printed in the 
originai languages, opposite to the Pac-similes — ^line for line^without contrac- 
tions — ^thus facilitating reference and aiding effectively those interested in 
pal»ograpbìc studies. 

In the work are also printed in full, for the first time, many originai and 
important histoiical documents.
